# Authoring Lessons Log

> **Status**: Accepted | **Updated**: 2026-07-25
> **Serves**: [A11](./capability-register.md) (structural quality tools across the corpus)
> **Validated by**: `uv run python scripts/check_lessons_log.py`

<!-- -->

> **Note on this first landing.** The lessons below were learned building a
> three-book 16+ gamebook series (the Wyrmreach chain), and several `Ref` cells
> cite that series' spec, skeleton, and filled-story artifacts. Those artifacts
> are **not** part of this change: they land in a separate follow-up push so the
> process and tooling work can be reviewed on its own, alongside the other
> in-flight revisions to story development. Every lesson and every tooling change
> here stands without them; the story-dependent tests skip until they arrive
> (6 skips in the suite today), and the two Wyrmreach design and report documents
> are included because they are the evidence base each lesson cites.

Every story development run appends here. The point is that a lesson learned once becomes a
**change to the tooling** rather than folklore an author has to rediscover, so each row carries a
proposed change and a status that tracks whether it landed.

## How to use this log

**When to append.** At the end of any authoring run: a new story or series book, a skeleton
promotion, a fill pass, or a validator/gate change made in service of authoring. If the run
produced no lesson, append nothing; an empty run is a real outcome.

**What counts as a lesson.** Something a future author or a future tooling change should act on.
Three tests, any one of which qualifies it:

1. It cost real iteration to discover (a rule interaction, a calibration, an ordering constraint).
2. The tooling let a defect through, or reported it in a way that did not point at the cause.
3. It would be re-learned from scratch by the next person, because nothing in the repo records it.

A one-off typo is not a lesson. "The gate reports band membership, so the floor reads as a pass"
is.

**Fields.**

| Field | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| `ID` | `AL-NNN`, sequential, never reused or renumbered. |
| `Date` | ISO date the lesson was recorded. |
| `Source` | The run that produced it (story slug, series book, or workstream). |
| `Category` | One of `validator`, `tooling`, `authoring-craft`, `scale`, `metadata`, `process`, `docs`, `product`. |
| `Lesson` | What is true, stated so it is actionable without reading the source run. |
| `Proposed change` | The change that would stop this recurring, or `none (craft note)` when the lesson is guidance rather than a code change. |
| `Status` | `open`, `accepted`, `applied`, `rejected`, or `superseded`. |
| `Ref` | Commit, PR, file, or doc. **Required** when status is `applied`, `rejected`, or `superseded`. |

**Review cadence.** Sweep the `open` rows whenever a phase is planned. An `open` row that has
survived two phase boundaries is either genuinely low value (mark it `rejected`, with the reason in
`Ref`) or it is real debt being avoided; either way, decide rather than let it sit.

**Status discipline.** `accepted` means the change is agreed but unbuilt, and it must appear in a
plan somewhere. `applied` means the change is merged and the `Ref` proves it. Nothing moves to
`applied` on the strength of intent.

| AL-001 | 2026-07-25 | wyrmreach b1-b3 | tooling | Compiling the FILL skeleton and the filled story from one spec makes drift between them structurally impossible; two hand-maintained artifacts drift silently. | Keep one-spec compilation as the authoring pattern for any multi-artifact story. | applied | `scripts/build_series_book.py` |
| AL-002 | 2026-07-25 | wyrmreach b2 | authoring-craft | In a `carries_state: true` continuation a carried variable initialises `true`, so every `== false` condition on it is unsatisfiable and a hard L2-11. Carried state must be read-only; acquisition moves to a new book-local variable. | none (craft note); recorded in the series design doc's carried-variable rule. | applied | `wyrmreach-series-design.md` section 4 |
| AL-003 | 2026-07-25 | wyrmreach b2, b3 | validator | A gate on a *new* variable placed earlier in the graph than any node granting it is also an L2-11 dead branch. | AL-004 | 2026-07-25 | wyrmreach b1-b3 | authoring-craft | Flesch-Kincaid is driven by sentence count, not passage length: at a fixed ~60 words the same content scores FK 5 in four or five sentences, 9-11 in three, 13+ in two. The practical 16+ rule is three sentences per passage. | none (craft note); recorded in the series design doc's reading-level section. | applied | `wyrmreach-series-design.md` section 6 |
| AL-005 | 2026-07-25 | wyrmreach b3 | authoring-craft | An RL-13 outlier can be retuned without rewriting a word by joining or splitting sentence boundaries; 185 of 212 cleared this way. The largest is now 746. Not a live bug: `visit_set` resets per read-through (`player/engine.ts::start`) and shrinks on undo (`back()` replays from the start), and book 3's depth is 74, so a real read never approaches 505. Nothing tests the constant against the catalog, so the false basis is invisible. | Update the derivation and the number to the real largest skeleton, and add a lockstep test asserting the constant matches the largest `production_eligible` skeleton in `skeletons/`, so the next oversized book fails a test instead of quietly invalidating a comment. | AL-025 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | process | An adversarial review is only as good as its ability to finish. | AL-026 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | authoring-craft | **Breadth built from terminating leaves halves the typical read.** Reproduced with `player/engine.StoryEngine` over 3,000 condition-aware random reads: book 1 (305 nodes) has a median read of 11 pages / 650 words; book 3 (746 nodes) has a median of **5 pages / 302 words** of a 42,085-word book. Termination density is identical (22.3% vs 22.5% of choices land straight on an ending), so the extra 441 nodes went into *width made of failure leaves*, not into reader-visible journey. 7 endings sit within two taps of the start, 3 decision nodes have every choice end the book, and 3,000 random reads reached only 94 of 232 endings. This qualifies AL-009: hanging rooms off an act hub does control depth, but paying for breadth in terminating leaves makes the book *feel smaller* the bigger it gets. | Author breadth as reconvergent detours, not terminal leaves: a room should more often cost `vigor` and rejoin than end the run. PL-17's endings floor actively pushes toward terminal leaves at gamebook breadth, so the floor and this lesson are in tension and the floor's shape should be revisited. | applied | 45 shallowest failure leaves converted to vigor-costing pass-throughs; median read 5 -> 20 pages, endings reached 39% -> 56%; gate clean, chain clean, endings exactly on the 187 floor |
| AL-027 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | validator | Nothing constrains the **typical** path length. PL-20 floors only the *fastest satisfying* path (37 nodes for this cell), which book 3 satisfies at 52, while the median actual read is 5 pages. A book can pass every scale rule and still hand most readers a 90-second encounter. | Add a scale advisory on expected read length over the walk machinery `validator/walk.py` already has: warn when the median uniform-walk playthrough falls below a per-cell page floor, or when more than ~20% of a node's choices terminate. Roughly 20 lines on existing infrastructure, and it would have caught AL-026 before the fill pass. | open | `validator/walk.py`, `validator/policy.py` |
| AL-028 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | product | K6's "found N of M endings" replay motivator inverts at large M. At M=232 a finished read reports "1 of 232" (0.4%), and `EndingsBadge` silently drops its dot row above `MAX_DOTS = 10`, removing the only kid-legible part of the mechanic precisely for the books that most need replay motivation. Part of the denominator is also effectively unreachable: 3,000 random reads found 94 of 232. | Stop showing a raw denominator above a threshold: milestone framing ("3 new endings found, lots more to go") with a tier ladder, or scope the denominator to a curated signature-endings subset. `EndingsProgress` and `EndingsBadge` must share the threshold so the ending screen and the shelf never disagree. | open | `frontend/src/reader/EndingsProgress.tsx:60-65`, `frontend/src/library/EndingsBadge.tsx:19-40` |
| AL-029 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | product | The reader progress bar is `visit_set.length / story.nodes.length`, so it measures corpus coverage, not route progress. The numeric label is already suppressed as untrustworthy, but the `aria-label` still announces "5 of 746 pages explored". | Make in-read progress route-relative (path length against distance-to-nearest-ending from the current node, precomputable from the walk the validator already does), or show "Page 5" for large books. Corpus coverage belongs on the shelf card, not in the reader chrome. | open | `frontend/src/reader/readerProgress.ts:7-11`, `ReaderChrome.tsx:88` |
| AL-030 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | product | Every page turn synchronously replays the entire read from page 1: `canGoBack` calls `back()`, which DFS-replays the recorded path, and discards everything but a boolean. Compounding it, `nodeIndex` rebuilds a 746-entry Map on every engine entry point, so a depth-71 replay performs ~285 rebuilds. Measured O(depth x nodes): 26 ms at depth 70 on a container CPU, and a BACK press pays it three times, so a phone plausibly freezes 300ms+ on the one control built for children who mis-tap. Cost grows with the depth the book is designed to reward. | Memoize the node index and `intBounds` per Storybook in a `WeakMap` (story identity is stable for the reader's lifetime, satisfying the existing `#ASSUME`); stop `canGoBack` recomputing an answer it throws away; short-circuit the common "no" case off the render path. | open | `frontend/src/player/engine.ts:17-19,325-347`, `reader/Reader.tsx:216`, `player/machine.ts:99-111` |
| AL-031 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | product | No response compression is configured anywhere in the stack (no `GZipMiddleware` in `app.py`, nothing in either compose file), so a story download ships **405 KB instead of 107 KB** and the 3-book series costs 750 KB instead of 202 KB. Separately, `list_library` and `/v1/reading-history` `select(StorybookVersion)` whole-row including the `blob` JSONB for every book, decoding 768 KB of prose per shelf render to produce three small cards; the reading-history call fires on every ending-screen mount, which at a 5-page median read is constantly. | Add `GZipMiddleware(minimum_size=1024)`, a one-line ~4x win on the one step where a child waits with nothing to do. Separately, denormalise `title`, `node_count`, `ending_count` and `ending_node_ids` onto `StorybookVersion` at publish time so the listing paths never load prose. | applied | `GZipMiddleware(minimum_size=1024)` in `app.py`; the listing-path denormalisation half stays open under this id |
| AL-032 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | product | Reconnecting mid-read can teleport a child backwards to an earlier page and remount the reader, from **this device's own queued past**. `persist()` updates `revisionRef` only on `saved`, never on `queued`, so after offline reading the live save carries a stale base revision; the replay-on-reconnect advances the server revision, the next tap 409s, and the 409 handler adopts the server row unconditionally and bumps `readerKey` to remount. The existing `#ASSUME` deliberately accepts losing position to *another device*, but explicitly reasons about "another device advanced this story's row" and does not contemplate this case. A 74-choice offline read queues 74 sequential writes, so the 409 window is proportional to how much the child read offline. Traced end to end, not executed. | Collapse the offline queue to the newest state per (profile, storybook) before replay so 74 round-trips become 1; advance `revisionRef` on `queued`; and guard the adopt branch so a `current_row` that is a strict prefix of the local path is recognised as this device's own stale past and re-saved rather than adopted. | open | `frontend/src/reader/ReaderPage.tsx:296,304-305,317-337`, `offline/sync.ts:277-325`, `api/reading.py:363-371` |
| AL-033 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | validator | **Safety hole.** `_screen_all_nodes` calls the bright-line classifiers once per node in a sequential loop guarded by `if openai_key and openai_reason is None`, so the FIRST `ClassifierUnavailable` disables that classifier **for every remaining node**, with no retry and no per-node coverage record. At 746 nodes Stage 0 makes 1,492 sequential third-party calls, which is exactly the shape that trips a Perspective ~1 QPS quota or an OpenAI per-minute limit; a 429 at node 50 leaves 93% of the book unscreened for `sexual/minors`, `self-harm/instructions` and `illicit/violent`. The resulting report is indistinguishable from a fully-screened clean one apart from one whole-story ADVISORY, and `summary.hard_block`/`soft_flag` are unaffected, so `submit` and `approve` both pass. Verified by reading the loop; the docstring states the no-retry behaviour outright. | Track per-node classifier coverage and emit a **FLAG**, not an advisory, naming the unscreened node count and ids, so the story cannot reach `in_review` clean. Add bounded retry with backoff plus a concurrency limit so a rate limit is absorbed rather than abandoning the book. Make `submit`/`approve` refuse a report declaring incomplete Stage-0 coverage. | applied | `moderation/classifiers.py`: per-node coverage, bounded retry, circuit breaker, and a gating `classifier_coverage_incomplete` FLAG; 6 new tests |
| AL-034 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | scale | One import of this book is ~2,986 strictly sequential provider round trips (2 classifier calls plus 2 review calls per node, plus 2 whole-story stages), with no `gather`, semaphore, batching or call budget, all inside **one** Postgres transaction that also holds `SELECT FOR UPDATE` on the Storybook row. That is a 40 to 100 minute `idle in transaction` session against a pooler configured for `transaction` mode, and any single failure discards the entire run with no checkpoint or resume. | Bound the per-node stages with a concurrency semaphore and a per-story call/time budget that records an explicit "moderation incomplete" finding when exceeded. Split the import: commit the draft first, then moderate in short transactions, persisting findings incrementally so a retry re-screens only unscreened nodes. `approve` already refuses a version with no moderation report, so a committed unmoderated draft is safe to leave behind. | open | `moderation/stages.py:196-268`, `pipeline.py:98`, `generation/import_cli.py:92-119` |
| AL-035 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | scale | `run_gate` is a measured 3.72 second synchronous CPU burn on this book and is called inline from three `async def` handlers with no `to_thread`, so it stalls the whole event loop: every passage edit, an admin rescreen sweep, and `validate_version`, which is guardian-reachable and therefore a cheap self-DoS on the entire API. | Wrap all three in `anyio.to_thread.run_sync`, cache the gate report per `(storybook_id, version)` so `validate_version` returns the stored report, and rate-limit it. | applied | `anyio.to_thread.run_sync` around `run_gate` in `api/node_edit.py`, `api/generation.py`, `moderation/rescreen.py` |
| AL-036 | 2026-07-25 | adversarial review | product | ADR-005 makes a human's recorded approval the only path to a child, and at 746 nodes that surface cannot deliver it: the review view ships the whole blob (measured 444 KB), renders all 746 passages, 232 ending badges and 1,030 choice rows unvirtualized in one DOM, and offers no pagination, no per-node reviewed state, no resume, and one Approve button at the bottom. A reviewer cannot read 42,085 words in one page load, so the recorded approval attests to far less than it claims. | AL-094 | 2026-08-06 | PR #635 review, the PL-26 corridor guard | validator | One computed shortest path was shared by two rules, and the sharing was documented as the safety property: "PL-20 and PL-26 both read this one result, so they can never disagree about which walk is the fastest finish". PL-26 reads the DECISION COUNT along the path, which is not invariant, so `_shortest_path_to`'s tie-break (`sorted(graph.successors(...))`, i.e. node-id alphabetical order) silently became part of the rule's semantics: renaming two routes flipped the verdict from 7.0 nodes per decision (WARNING) to 3.5 and SILENT, with zero structural change. Determinism was mistaken for correctness; it guarantees the same arbitrary answer every run, not the right one. Blast radius measured by sweeping the catalog: 19 of 58 PL-26-eligible skeletons had their density mismeasured, a third of the corpus, and 2 were under-reported across the ceiling. | `_fewest_decision_shortest_path`, a layered dynamic program over the shortest-path DAG (BFS levels, then one forward sweep where edge u->v is in the DAG exactly when level[v] == level[u]+1), returning the minimum-decision path among the minimum-length ones. Worst case rather than canonical case, because PL-26 is a CEILING and must fire when ANY equally fast walk is a corridor. O(V+E), deliberately not enumeration: shortest-path count is exponential (a chain of parallel two-way detours doubles it per link) and this runs in the request path. `_decision_node_ids` extracted so PL-25, PL-26 and the search cannot drift on what a decision is. Generalised check: when two rules share one derived artifact, ask which PROPERTIES of it each reads. Sharing is safe only while every reader depends on an INVARIANT of the artifact; the moment one reader depends on a property that varies among equally valid artifacts, the tie-break has become that rule's semantics and must be chosen by the rule, not inherited from the search. State the invariant in the docstring instead of citing the sharing. | applied | `validator/policy.py` `_fewest_decision_shortest_path` / `_decision_node_ids` / `_breadth_first_levels`; verified against a brute-force `nx.all_simple_paths` oracle over 2,614 random digraphs, 0 mismatches on walk validity, endpoints, minimum length, and minimum decisions |

Working fix (authoring-only, no validator or design-doc change): pair every stat-gated choice with an auxiliary ordinary (non-canonical) boolean `resolve`, reset by an unconditioned binary player choice immediately before the gate, and XOR-combine it with the stat threshold so the pair collapses to depend only on `resolve` at the fixed baseline value (both branches reachable via ordinary play, satisfying `L2-11`) while genuinely differentiating on the stat at other envelope states (for `CH-3a`/`CH-3b`/`CH-4`'s per-state walks). Applied across a whole book it made all 14 stations mechanically identical, and the pilot read as scaffolding rather than a gamebook, so it was withdrawn from the branch on 2026-08-08 and preserved out of tree rather than shipped. A cost model that measures only configuration count therefore missed the cost that mattered: the workaround is free in walk time and expensive in reader-perceptible agency, and nothing in the gate could have reported the second. | Two parts, and the second is the real decision. | AL-135 | 2026-08-08 | PR #649 CI, persistent-characters runtime (branch `feat/persistent-characters-runtime`) | tooling | A schema invariant recorded only as a RAD prose comment is enforced by nothing, so the branch that falsifies it finds out from a downstream job rather than from a test. `scripts/reset_e2e_real_state.py` issued a bare `TRUNCATE TABLE reading_state` under an `#ASSUME` stating that nothing declares a foreign key onto that table. Two compounding causes: the invariant lived in prose, which cannot fail; and the `#VERIFY` it cited, `test_reset_e2e_real_state_truncates_reading_state`, did not exist and was grandfathered in `rad-citation-baseline.toml`, so the new RAD citation gate correctly stayed silent. Note also that `ondelete="CASCADE"` does not rescue a `TRUNCATE` the way it rescues a `DELETE`, so a correctly declared foreign key still breaks the statement. | Convert the prose invariant into a schema-derived test: list the truncated tables in one constant and assert, from live SQLAlchemy metadata, that every table with a foreign key onto a member of that set is itself a member. Done here as `test_truncated_tables_cover_every_dependent_foreign_key`, break-tested against the pre-fix single-table set (fails naming `character_book_completion`) and the fixed set (passes). Prefer naming each table explicitly over `TRUNCATE ... CASCADE`, which converts this loud Postgres error into silent data loss for the next foreign key someone adds. Generalises past this one script: any RAD comment that asserts a whole-schema property is a candidate for a metadata-derived test, and any grandfathered baseline citation is a claim currently proved by nothing. | open | `UW-C77` |
| AL-136 | 2026-08-08 | PR #649 CI, persistent-characters runtime (branch `feat/persistent-characters-runtime`) | process | A gate placed where the data was convenient rather than where the need is silently becomes a product-wide behaviour change, and a green unit suite cannot see it. `KidShell` already fetched the active character for the kid library route, so the first-run character gate was written there: with `status === 'none'` it rendered `CharacterCreator` instead of the library `Outlet`. Every existing child was hard-gated into creating a character before reaching their library, with no skip affordance, for a feature no catalog book can use, which also contradicted the branch's own stated posture that the chain merges enforced and unreached. Six unit tests covered the gate and all six passed, because each asserted the gate did what it was built to do; none could ask whether the scope was right. The only thing that caught it was the real-backend Playwright smoke, and it surfaced as a 30s timeout on `getByRole('link', {name: 'The Tide Pool Mystery'})`, a locator that says nothing about characters. | Two changes. First, when a plan says "show X when Y has none", make it state the SCOPE explicitly, per book, per profile, or per route, because the implementer will otherwise put the check wherever the data already is, and that location silently sets the scope. Second, treat "changes what every existing user sees on next visit" as a distinct review question the diff cannot answer: a per-task reviewer reading only the gate sees a correct gate. Resolved here by exposing `accepts_character` on `LibraryItem` and deciding at the book, at click time, failing open on every non-'none' status since the backend already treats an unseeded read as normal. | open | `UW-C78` |
| AL-137 | 2026-08-08 | PR #649 CI, persistent-characters runtime (branch `feat/persistent-characters-runtime`) | tooling | A pull request that conflicts with its base runs ZERO GitHub Actions workflows, and the third-party app checks that remain make it read as green. `pull_request` workflows are evaluated against `refs/pull/N/merge`, a ref GitHub cannot create while the branch conflicts, so after `origin/main` moved under this branch the second push produced no runs at all. What `gh pr checks` then showed was four checks, all passing: CodeRabbit, GitGuardian, Socket Security, and qlty. Those post against the head SHA through the Checks API and are unaffected by the merge ref, so they keep reporting on a branch whose real CI never started. Nothing in that output says "conflicted" or "no runs"; the only tells are the context COUNT collapsing from ~30 to 4, and `gh run list --branch <b>` showing its newest head SHA behind the PR's. This is the same family as the existing roll-up traps (a skipped job renders green, a job with no runner renders red with `steps: []`), but strictly worse, because here the roll-up check does not exist at all rather than reporting the wrong colour. | Never read `gh pr checks` alone as evidence that CI ran. Check `gh pr view <n> --json mergeable,mergeStateStatus` in the same call and treat `CONFLICTING`/`DIRTY` as "CI is not running", not as "merge later"; and cross-check the newest head SHA in `gh run list --branch <b>` against the PR head SHA, since a matching SHA is the only positive evidence the push was picked up. Worth folding into whatever check-verification helper the PR workflow settles on, as a precondition asserted before any check state is interpreted. | open | `UW-C79` |

| AL-188 | 2026-08-09 | Pre-rebuild review, strict-mode implementation (branch `claude/skeleton-story-review-3zy6tq`) | tooling | `load_skeleton` ran the full gate and then threw the `ValidationReport` away unless it blocked, so `check_skeleton.py`, the tool every authoring wave gates shells with, printed a clean ok for skeletons the gate had warned about. The family was designed against filled stories and nothing documented which CG rules are shell-safe, so the first strict run failed the demo fixture on ten findings of which six were structurally meaningless. | AL-190 | 2026-08-09 | Reconvergence hard-gate ruling implementation (branch `claude/skeleton-story-review-3zy6tq`) | validator | A reconvergence cap designed from the aggregate (corpus mean max in-degree 7.79 vs the research anchor 1.5) would have banned two whole topology families, because the aggregate mixes two opposite mechanisms. A blanket per-band cap tight enough to catch the funnels excludes every open map; one loose enough to admit open maps catches nothing. The gate had to be topology-aware to mean anything: cap `branch_and_bottleneck`/`gauntlet`/`sorting_hat`/`time_cave` at 4/4/6/6/8/8 by band, exempt the hub topologies whose re-entry is the design, and leave those to the SQ-15 per-path metrics. The general shape: a structural quality gate calibrated on a corpus-wide distribution silently encodes the assumption that one mechanism produced the distribution. | Applied: topology-aware max in-degree hard cap in `check_skeleton.py --strict` with the exemption rationale in-code; when the strict checks are promoted into `validator/` after the grandfathered catalog is removed, carry the topology awareness into the catalogued rule rather than flattening it to one number. | open | delivered on the story-diversity work, currently OPEN on PR #661; the earlier SHAs live only on a scratch branch that will never merge, so this flips to `applied` on the merge commit |

The general shape: an inert gate is not a deferred decision, it is a debt meter running with the display off, and the census that reads the meter costs one afternoon against a rebuild-sized principal. | Applied for this flip: the census ran before any removal and its numbers plus a remove-on-replacement sequencing recommendation are recorded in the 2026-08-09 review (Part 4 census section), so the removal wave is sized by data. | AL-327 | 2026-08-12 | Tracing why four vendor-comparison books were byte-identical to their skeletons | tooling | **When a fill produces no parseable document the orchestrator seeds the repair loop with the unfilled skeleton, and if repair makes no progress it returns that skeleton as a passing book.** `orchestrator.py:1000` sets `last_valid_doc = current_doc if current_doc is not None else skeleton`, and `:1013` seeds repair from it, so a `None` fill (invalid JSON, non-dict, truncated output) enters repair as the authoring skeleton. Truncation is the upstream cause and this fallback is what hides it. See AL-328. | AL-328 | 2026-08-12 | Diagnosing why two vendor-comparison legs returned unfilled books | tooling | **A fixed `max_tokens` is not a neutral condition in a cross-vendor comparison, because models differ roughly fivefold in completion budget for identical work, so one cap silently converts a verbose model into a failing one.** Measured at the production cap of 32,000 on the same skeleton and brief: `openai/gpt-5.6-sol` spent 6,054 completion tokens, `google/gemini-3-flash-preview` 6,506, `anthropic/claude-sonnet-5` 29,252, and `moonshotai/kimi-k3` all 32,000 with 28,247 of them on hidden reasoning. The two legs the comparison scored as unreliable are exactly the two with no headroom, and Sonnet 5's `fill_completeness` of 0.25 is a property of `_MAX_TOKENS_PROSE`, not of Anthropic. The relationship is dose-response rather than anecdotal: ranking Sonnet 5's four briefs by budget consumed gives 100, 91, 83 and 78 percent of cap, and the single brief it filled in run-1 is the cheapest of the four. Cost spreads even wider than tokens, twelvefold, at 2.9 cents against 34 cents for one book. | Report the cap as a measured condition of any cross-vendor comparison, and refuse to publish a leg's fill rate when its completions land within a stated margin of the cap; a leg running at 91 percent of budget is a leg whose next run is a coin toss, so its number describes the harness. Size the production cap from the most budget-hungry supported model plus its reasoning overhead rather than from the size of a book, which is AL-323 restated at whole-book scale. Generalised check: before attributing a failure to the thing under test, confirm the test's own limits are not the binding constraint, especially where one limit is shared by legs that consume it at very different rates. | applied | Reproduced 2026-08-12 with direct OpenRouter calls at `max_tokens=32000` over `skeletons/5-8/*` and `docs/planning/vendor-comparison/briefs-5-8.json`: Sonnet 5 on brief 1 returns `finish_reason='length'` at exactly 32,000 completion tokens with JSON that fails to decode, matching its 1,001-second run-1 latency against 329 to 374 seconds on its other briefs, while briefs 0, 2 and 3 return `'stop'` at 91, 83 and 78 percent of cap. Kimi K3 returns `'length'` with `reasoning_tokens=28247` and 14,030 characters of truncated JSON. Corrects AL-327's inference that truncation was ruled out. Applied 2026-08-13: `BookRecord.max_tokens` stamped on every row, `near_cap` suppresses a fill rate for any leg spending 95 percent of its budget, and `_print_conditions` warns when one report mixes caps; `test_a_leg_with_no_budget_headroom_is_flagged_rather_than_rated` |
| AL-329 | 2026-08-12 | Diagnosing why two vendor-comparison legs returned unfilled books | tooling | **`GenerationProvider.complete` returns a bare string, so the `usage` object is discarded at the provider boundary and two opposite failures reach the caller as one indistinguishable sentence.** A reasoning model that burned the whole budget before emitting a token and an endpoint that answered with nothing both surface as `openrouter response had no message content`. The first wants a larger cap and the second wants the leg dropped, and the log line cannot tell them apart, so the comparison retried Kimi K3 three times at roughly eleven minutes and 50 cents per attempt against a cause no retry could fix. The single field that separates them, `finish_reason`, plus `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`, is already present in every OpenRouter response and thrown away. Diagnosing this required bypassing the pipeline entirely and calling the vendor by hand. | Surface `finish_reason` and the reasoning-token count on the provider result and include them in the transient-retry log line, and do not retry a completion that stopped on `length`, since retrying a budget failure at the same budget cannot succeed. This is the diagnostic half of the usage capture #701 adds for cost; cost is the lesser of the two uses. Generalised check: when an adapter narrows a rich vendor response to one convenient type, ask which failure modes become indistinguishable downstream, and whether any retry policy keys off a distinction that no longer exists. | applied | Observed 2026-08-12: `out/vendor-comparison/` run logs record `provider.transient_retry attempt=1..3 error='openrouter response had no message content'` for `openrouter:moonshotai/kimi-k3`, then `provider.retries_exhausted`. The same call issued directly returns HTTP 200 with `finish_reason='length'`, `completion_tokens=32000` and `reasoning_tokens=28247`, none of which is reachable from `complete`'s `str` return. Applied 2026-08-13: `dig_finish_reason`/`dig_reasoning_tokens` in the adapter base, `Completion.finish_reason` and `TokenUsage.reasoning_tokens`, and an empty body at `finish_reason=length` is leg-fatal rather than retried; `test_a_completion_truncated_at_the_budget_is_not_retried` |

| AL-338 | 2026-08-12 | Building the W1 covering-set path enumerator and running it over the real skeleton catalogue | tooling | **Enumerate-and-filter is the wrong shape for a covering set, and a budget-capped depth-first search reports an honest coverage number that is useless.** The first implementation enumerated root-to-ending readings depth first under a 20,000-reading cap and kept each one that covered a new choice. On books with more readings than budget it spent the entire budget inside whichever subtree it entered first, so `16+/the-tenfold-siege` reported 30.5% edge coverage; 35 of 61 catalogue books came in below full coverage and 24 reported themselves incomplete. Nothing was lying: the number was exactly what the algorithm achieved. The defect is that coverage was a *residue of the search* rather than a *constructed property*, so it inherited the search's arbitrary order. Rebuilding it as targeted construction (index the configuration closure once, forward-BFS for a shortest route in, backward-BFS from endings for a shortest route out, then splice an O(1) reading through each still-uncovered choice) moved all 61 books to 1.000 coverage and cut whole-catalogue time to 2.666 s, because cost now scales with the configuration graph rather than with the reading count. A 55-node book can admit 262,144 distinct readings; any algorithm whose cost tracks that number fails at catalogue scale by construction. | When a metric must be *guaranteed* rather than *estimated*, construct one witness per target instead of filtering an enumeration, and treat any coverage figure produced under a budget cap as a statement about the budget until proven otherwise. | AL-365 | 2026-08-13 | Building W3's fork-consequence measure and watching it report "not measured" over the whole 61-book catalogue | validator | **A search that ends because it ran out of graph and a search that ends because it ran out of budget are the same empty result, and collapsing them turned a working measure into silence.** W3 walks both branches of a fork looking for where they rejoin. That is correct for a horizon hit and wrong for the commonest shape in the corpus: branches that run to different endings never rejoin, and never rejoining is the *most* consequential thing a fork can do. The outcome was foreseeable: paraphrase detection is entailment, and no lexical or semantic resource is in the dependency set (`nltk`, `spacy`, `gensim`, `scikit-learn` all absent). Building the candidate anyway took under an hour and produced something the prediction could not: a runnable demonstration that the strongest purely lexical form of the check passes three easy cases, survives reordering, and misses "the man who tended the light had been taking the cargo" against a declared "the lighthouse keeper is the thief", two sentences sharing no content word. | AL-398 | 2026-08-14 | Checking both readers' flagship findings before writing either into a ticket | process | **A frontier reader's converged PATTERN is trustworthy and its INSTANCES are not, and the cost of confusing the two is a ticket built on a defect that does not exist.** Two readers, different book sets, no contact, named the same five classes; that convergence is the strongest evidence available that these are properties of the corpus rather than of a reader's taste. The instances held up far worse. Reader A's flagship was REFUTED outright: all 15 paths to `the-night-market`'s `f_string`, `e_up_chorus`, `f_lantern` and `f_friends` pass through a node depicting the writing and folding of the wish, so the state it claimed was unestablished is established on every path. Reader B's flagship was verified and provable from metadata alone. Of the safety class's strongest instances, two carried adult supervision in the same node the report quoted. So of four checked instances, one was wrong, two were overstated, and one was exactly right, while the classes they belonged to were all real. The asymmetry has a cause worth naming: a reader reasons over the text in front of it and cannot enumerate paths, so it is reliable about what a book DOES and unreliable about what every route through the book does. | Treat a reader review as a hypothesis generator with a mandatory verification step, and write that into the brief rather than leaving it to the supervisor's judgement: every instance carries a node id and a quote so it CAN be checked, and no instance reaches a register row until it has been. Where the claim is about paths, check it with `validator/paths.py` rather than by re-reading. Generalised, and the converse of `AL-367`: a deterministic measure must not arbitrate before its accuracy is checked, and a judged one must not be dismissed for getting instances wrong when the pattern replicates across independent readers. | open | Both flagships checked directly against the book JSON; reported in `out/reviews/verification-fable-a.md` and `out/reviews/verification-fable-b-and-convergence.md`. Scheduled as `UW-C265` |
| AL-399 | 2026-08-14 | Fixing the syllable counter against an external answer key rather than against the bug report | validator | **A hand-rolled linguistic heuristic can be fixed properly in an afternoon once you stop guessing and fetch ground truth, and the guessing is what makes it look hard.** CMUdict gives an unambiguous syllable count for 115,901 words; scoring the counter against it turned every design question into a measurement. Dictionary-type accuracy went 83.03 to 90.32 percent, corpus-token accuracy 94.21 to 99.14 percent, and the FK grade bias, the number that actually reached the catalogue, went +0.268 to -0.031. Seven rules earned their place: silent `-ed` with a 19-word exception list derived from the dictionary rather than from memory, syllabic `-Cle` (and its `-Cled` inflection, which must not swallow `pulled`), silent `e` stranded by a consonant-initial suffix and stacked (``care` plus `ful` plus `ly``), `-es` silent except after a sibilant or vowel (`comes` 1, `faces` 2), vowel-before-`-ing` hiatus, `y` as a consonant between vowels, and three hiatus pairs. Just as important is what was measured and REJECTED: splitting `ea`, `ie` or `io` is the obvious next move and each one loses more words than it wins, because those pairs are majority-digraph in English (12, 17 and 28 percent hiatus). The counter therefore ships with `lion`, `poem` and `cereal` knowingly wrong, and the test file says so. | Reach for an external answer key before touching a heuristic, and prefer a free authoritative one over building a curated set by hand: 45 words took an hour to curate and 115,901 took one `curl`. Keep BOTH, because they answer different questions, the dictionary measuring generalisation and the corpus measuring what the error costs here. Report accuracy on a held-out split (both hash halves landed within 0.2 points, so the rules generalise rather than memorise). Generalised: the reason this defect survived so long is not that syllables are hard, it is that nobody had a way to be told they were wrong. | applied | `src/cyo_adventure/validator/reading_level.py` and `tests/unit/test_syllable_accuracy.py`; ground truth is CMUdict (`cmusphinx/cmudict`, `cmudict.dict`), fetched rather than vendored, and the corpus half is the 31 committed books' 390,334 tokens |
| AL-400 | 2026-08-14 | Measuring what the corrected counter does to the books that were written against the broken one | validator | **Fixing the measure moved every book in the catalogue, and the reported compliance FELL, because the corpus had been repaired until a biased number looked right.** All 31 committed books drop, mean -0.27 whole-book FK grades, range -0.73 to +0.29, and the mean in-band share falls from 92.5 to 86.8 percent with 25 of 31 books worse. The prose did not change; the books were always about a quarter of a grade easier than they declared, and the over-counting was propping them into band. The 10-13 band shifted most (-0.51 mean, n=4). Two books move a long way: `the-salt-archive` 99.6 to 77.4 percent and `the-sleepy-little-star` 100 to 76.5 percent. This has a live consequence beyond reporting: `generation/reading_level_loop.py` accepts a revision on `abs(after - target) < abs(before - target)`, which is symmetric, so with an accurate counter the loop will now push young-band prose HARDER to reach a target the old counter reached for free. The per-book `metadata.reading_level.target` values were declared at skeleton authoring and then validated by watching books converge on them, so they carry the counter's bias in exactly the way a calibration does. | Do NOT shift the targets to preserve the old numbers, which would bake the counting bug into the spec. Re-derive them instead, as a deliberate decision with the corrected counter in hand: state what FK grade each band should mean as a claim about readers, check it against the young-band floor problem (`target 1.0 +/- 1.0` puts the 3-5 floor at 0.0, while a well-formed 5-word monosyllabic sentence scores about -1.8, so ideal 3-5 prose can be flagged for being too easy), and only then decide whether the repair loop should be allowed to drive prose upward at all in the young bands. Until that decision is made, read a 3-5 or 5-8 in-band figure as provisional. | open | Measured across all 31 committed books before and after the `AL-399` fix, with each book scored against its own declared `target`/`tolerance`. | AL-401 | 2026-08-14 | Deriving the band guidance from the corpus once the counter could be trusted | authoring-craft | **Sentence length carries about three quarters of the reading-level spread and vocabulary carries almost none, which inverts the advice authors were giving each other.** Measured over the 4,173 committed nodes that land inside their own band under the corrected counter: mean words per sentence runs 5.7, 7.8, 12.7, 14.1, 18.4, 21.0 across the six bands, while mean syllables per word runs 1.21, 1.27, 1.29, 1.30, 1.30, 1.37. Through the FK formula those give +5.97 and +1.89 grades respectively from 3-5 to 16+. A 3-5 book and a 16+ book are built from words of nearly the same difficulty; what separates them is how many arrive at once. Two consequences. The guide's own sentence-length figures were wrong in the direction that matters, quoting 8-12 words for 5-8 against a measured 7.8 and 14-18 for 10-13 against 14.1, so an author writing to the guide's upper bound started out of band. | AL-402 | 2026-08-15 | Building `UW-C263`'s two-tier continuity check, and measuring all three formulations of it | validator | **The continuity defect two readers converged on is real, and it has no checker: three formulations were built and measured, and the two that read prose failed while the exact one flags 85 percent of the corpus.** Per-reference definite-noun-phrase checking, requiring every path to a definite reference to pass through a node introducing it, yields 3.48 findings per node, because bridging reference is ordinary English and a first mention with `the` is usually correct. The exact structural version, ancestors minus dominators over the node graph, flags 3,815 of 4,472 nodes, which is not a bug: after the first bottleneck almost every node has an ancestor the reader may have skipped, and that is what branching means. **What the exact version does separate is worth more than the checker would have been.** All six `time_cave` books report ZERO optional history across 236 nodes, because a pure branching tree never reconverges, so every reader arrives having read the same thing. Meanwhile all six `loop_and_grow` skeletons declare no state of any kind, no variables, no `on_enter`, no conditional choices, while carrying 2 to 14 multi-history nodes each, and three of the six have already produced a verified continuity defect. The schema itself forbids Tier 1 from declaring variables, and all six are Tier 1 at 3-5 or 5-8, so this is not six authors erring; it is a topology promising accumulation its tier cannot represent. | Ship the measure, not a rule, on the same footing as `consequence.py`, and move the remedy to the skeleton catalogue where it belongs: a `loop_and_grow` skeleton at Tier 1 should be rejected at promotion, or the topology should be reserved for Tier 2. Three failed formulations pointed at prose; the one that worked pointed at the catalogue. | applied | `src/cyo_adventure/validator/continuity.py` and `tests/unit/test_continuity.py`; the three measurements are reproducible over `out/*.filled.json` and `skeletons/*/*.json`. | AL-403 | 2026-08-15 | Recomputing W7's inter-judge agreement per criterion on within-book deltas | validator | **The retracted agreement figures were not merely unusable, they were hiding the one thing the panel most needed to know: which judge is the outlier, and on what.** The old method rounded each judge's mean across all seven criteria to an integer and ran unweighted kappa, giving +0.16, +0.58 and +0.14. And on `choice_quality`, gemini-3.1 correlates NEGATIVELY with both others (-0.15 and -0.02) while gpt-5.6 and grok-4.6 agree at +0.46. gemini-3.1 is the outlier on four of seven criteria, and its marginals show why: it uses the full 1 to 5 range while the other two compress into 2 to 4. That is exactly the scale-spread artefact the old pooled kappa was measuring instead of agreement, and it independently confirms the earlier finding that excluding gemini-3.1 dropped the noise floors 2 to 6 times. | Report agreement per criterion, on the paired design's own unit (the within-book delta), with an ordinal-appropriate coefficient, and ALWAYS print the marginal distributions beside it so a reader can tell a real disagreement from a scale artefact. Generalised: a summary statistic computed across heterogeneous sub-measures does not average their agreement, it destroys it, and the destruction looks like a finding. | AL-415 | 2026-08-16 | Reading W7 run 4's per-criterion spread against the single leg-mean figure it replaces | validator | **A panel's run-to-run stability differs fourfold between its own criteria, so one pooled noise figure describes none of them.** The previous estimate reported 0.217 mean absolute movement between runs, computed over leg means because a killed run had been salvaged from stdout and stdout carries only the mean across seven criteria. A pooled figure sits in the middle of two populations and misrepresents both: it overstates the noise of the reproducible criteria and understates it for the volatile ones, in the same number. | Report instrument noise at the granularity the decision rule consumes. The battery prints `KEEP` for it in the same words it uses for `imagery`, whose effect is -2.00 with zero control movement, so the report flattens a fourfold difference in margin into one label. A first pass at this analysis also compared control noise against the *noise floor* rather than against the *effect size*, which produced a confident and wrong reading that `choice_quality` was the fragile one; the two quantities answer different questions and are easy to swap when both are printed as small decimals beside each other. | Print the margin, not just the side of it: report each verdict with its effect size and the noise it cleared, so a KEEP that passes by equality is visibly different from one that passes by 8x. Re-measured with the narrowest legal fan plus the CG-2 variance allowance, `5-8/short` moves to BUILDABLE at 26 nodes against a 50-node budget, and the quoted figures become 857 at 8-11/short (was 1,640), 1,764,165 at 10-13/long (was 6,457,008) and 1.6e14 at 16+/long/gamebook (was 9.4e19). Two of the three reached strict in one restructure each. Both restructures were the same move for the same reason: CG-2 caps a decision node at 3 options at 5-8, so `the-night-market`'s seven-lane market square and `the-tide-pool-rescue`'s four-quest hub had to become chains of three-way stops, which also broke the single-choice runs CG-1 was flagging, because an inserted hub is itself a decision node. Deleting branches to fit the bound would have removed four of six stalls and one of four quests, so the chain is what preserves the story. Two traps found by doing it: the `Q*_SIGN` personalization slots lived only in the deleted bridge nodes' choice LABELS, so a naive delete would have orphaned three contract slots; and lengthening the fastest route pushed the declared clock to 22 percent drift against PL-23's 25 percent tolerance, passing while being wrong. | Record the hub-chain as the standard CG-2 repair for a wide-fan `open_map` or `loop_and_grow`, and add two checks to any node-removing repair: assert no personalization slot loses its last use, and recompute the declared clock rather than assuming a structural change left it alone. Generalised check: triage legacy cells by violation count before deciding to author, because the distribution is extremely skewed and the cheap tail is worth taking first; and when removing nodes, the thing most likely to break is whatever was referenced from a field nobody thought to grep, which for this catalog is choice labels rather than bodies. | applied | `skeletons/5-8/the-tide-pool-rescue.json` 57 to 55 nodes and `skeletons/5-8/the-night-market.json` 59 to 60 nodes, both passing `check_skeleton.py --strict`; `estimated_minutes` corrected 7 to 9 on the latter; in-cell structural distances re-measured at 0.28 to 0.39 against a 0.05 floor |
| AL-447 | 2026-08-17 | Story-first experiment: a strong model was asked for the best 5-8 story it could write, given the age rules and no skeleton, and the result was then gated | product | **A writer optimising for story quality builds scenes; the framework counts beats, and almost the whole apparent conflict is that one difference of unit.** The story came back as 16 passages, median 253 words, 8 endings, max depth 5, fan exactly 2 everywhere. Gated as 5-8 it fails on two things: every single passage is over the 155-word per-node ceiling (178 to 301), and its fastest satisfying arc is 5 nodes against a floor of 7. Both are the same disagreement seen twice. Measured: re-cut the identical 3,944 words at the band's own 70-word mean and it implies about 56 nodes at roughly depth 18, which lands inside 5-8/medium's 50-86 budget and clears its arc floor of 9. So the story is not structurally deficient and does not need rewriting, it needs decomposing; a 'node' in this framework is a stop, and nothing in the authoring guidance says so to someone who has not read the validator. **The one conflict that is real is convergence.** The story is a pure tree, zero merges, every ending reached by its own path, and the writer volunteered that funnelling the paths into one shared scene 'would have been cheap' and wrote each ending fresh instead. That is the exact opposite of what `AL-442` shows the strict bar requires: above 3-5 a no-merge tree cannot fit any cell's node budget, so reconvergence is effectively compulsory. Craft rejected, deliberately and with a reason, the thing the architecture mandates. | Split the two findings, because only one is a defect. First, write the unit down: state in the drafting guide and the skeleton brief that a node is a rendered stop of roughly the band's words-per-node mean, not a scene, and give an example of one scene decomposing into three or four nodes. A story-first draft then becomes a legitimate input via a decomposition pass rather than a rejected artifact, which is worth having because a story-first draft is measurably better at the things no gate measures. Second, put the convergence question to the owner rather than letting the node budget answer it silently: every ending currently reachable from a shared bottleneck is an ending some reader arrives at without having earned it in their own path, which is precisely the defect class `AL-443` keeps finding by hand. Generalised check: when a generative process and a validator disagree, establish whether they disagree about the content or about the units before changing either; and a constraint that is never stated to authors is enforced anyway, so it should be written where authors read, not only where the gate runs. | open | |
| AL-448 | 2026-08-17 | Authoring `the-blackout-week` immediately after logging `AL-443` | authoring-craft | **The bottleneck-coherence defect recurred in the very next bottleneck skeleton written, by the same author, one day after the lesson was logged, which shows the checklist entry is necessary and not sufficient.** `AL-443` recorded that a shared post-bottleneck ending can carry one track's fingerprints and that the fix is to walk one path per track. Writing the 10-13 skeleton with that lesson in hand, three of its nineteen endings still came out track-bound: a reader who spent the whole story on the school's server was handed 'The Rota Just Stops', which is the neighbour track's payoff, plus an ending built on the shop's notebook and one naming a lost freezer. Found only by walking the paths, exactly as before; the gate was green throughout. The reason it recurred is structural rather than careless: the shared act is written last, when the tracks are freshest in mind, and the most vivid concrete detail available to write with is whichever track was written most recently. | Move the check earlier and make it mechanical rather than a reminder to be careful: write the post-bottleneck layer FIRST, before any track, so it cannot borrow track-specific detail that does not exist yet; then write the tracks against it. Failing that, a lint is now clearly worth building rather than filing, since hand-checking has now failed once with the lesson in hand: flag any post-bottleneck node whose distinctive vocabulary overlaps one pre-bottleneck track and not the others. Generalised check: a lesson whose remedy is 'remember to check X' will be re-learned; the remedy has to change the order of work or run in a tool, or it is folklore with a row number. | applied | Three endings and their choice labels rewritten track-neutral in `skeletons/10-13/the-blackout-week.json` (`e_quiet`, `e_notebook`, `e_tired_out`); the write-the-bottleneck-first ordering is the proposed change and is NOT yet applied, carried by `UW-C274` |
| AL-449 | 2026-08-17 | Reading a census that was six rows stale without noticing | tooling | **Piping a measuring script into `head` killed it with SIGPIPE before it wrote its artifact, while the truncated output on screen looked complete and correct.** The census script prints a report and then writes `census.json`. Run as `census.py \| head -40` the printed report was entirely accurate, and the JSON it never reached was left over from a run two changes earlier. The stale file was then used to compute a per-cell table that disagreed with the very output printed above it, which is the only reason it was caught. This is `AL-427`'s failure mode with a new cause: not a number read off a listing, but a number read from an artifact whose writer had been killed. | Never pipe a script whose job includes writing an artifact; redirect to a file and read the file. Better, make the artifact self-dating: have the census stamp its own row count and the git SHA it measured into the JSON, and have any consumer assert those match the tree it is running against, so a stale artifact fails loudly instead of computing a plausible wrong table. Generalised check: any 'print AND write' script has a silent-truncation mode under a pipe, and the printed half is the half that looks trustworthy. | applied | Census re-run unpiped; the stale-artifact table was discarded and recomputed. The self-dating stamp is the proposed change and is not yet built, carried by `UW-C274` |
| AL-450 | 2026-08-17 | Building the 3-5 and 5-8 skeletons and having PL-18 reject the natural shape | validator | **At 3-5 and 5-8 a reconverging ACYCLIC graph has no legal topology at all, so the shape an author naturally reaches for cannot be labelled.** PL-18 admits a reconverging acyclic graph only as `branch_and_bottleneck` or `gauntlet`, and PL-29 permits neither at 3-5 (which allows `loop_and_grow` and `time_cave`) nor at 5-8 (which allows those plus `open_map`). Every one of those four requires either a pure tree or a genuine back-edge. So a layered graph that merely narrows and widens, which is the obvious shape and the one that fits the node budget, is unbuildable at the two youngest bands until a cycle is added. Both skeletons therefore carry back-edges. They earn their keep narratively, going back to the shelf to think again and returning to the allotment gate between lines of enquiry, but the cycle was forced by the rule interaction and then justified, which is the wrong order. | This is the young-band face of `AL-442` and belongs in the same fix: whatever states the reachable topologies per cell must say that 3-5 and 5-8 require a cycle or a tree, so an author designs for it rather than discovering it from a PL-18 rejection after the content is written. Generalised check: two tables maintained independently, one saying what a shape may be called and one saying what a band may declare, will eventually intersect in the empty set for some cell; the intersection should be computed and asserted non-empty for every offered cell, as a test. | open | |
| AL-451 | 2026-08-17 | Owner asked whether splitting the story-first draft into no-decision nodes would pass, which forced a re-measurement | validator | **A feasibility bound was computed from the wrong extreme, and it was wrong in the one cell where the answer mattered.** `AL-442` minimised the smallest legal no-reconvergence tree using the WIDEST legal fan, with a comment calling that the easiest case for the author. It is the hardest: width grows as fan to the power of levels, so the narrowest legal fan is what makes a tree fit. Re-measured correctly, the count of cells where PL-29 offers a tree topology the strict bar forbids is 15, not 16, and `5-8/short` moves from impossible to buildable at 26 nodes against a 50-node budget. Every other verdict survives, because the fan is pinned at exactly 3 from 8-11 upward and no choice of extreme rescues those, which is why the error stayed invisible: 15 of 16 spot-checks would have agreed with it. It was caught only because a question about a specific cell forced the specific number to be recomputed, and then a real skeleton was built to check it: the story-first draft re-cut at beat length is a pure `time_cave`, 44 nodes, max in-degree 1, and passes `--strict` at 5-8/short with zero findings. | When a bound is computed by minimising or maximising over a free parameter, assert the direction rather than asserting it in a comment: compute the bound at BOTH extremes and check the chosen one is actually the better, which is two lines and would have caught this immediately. Generalised check: a comment claiming 'this is the easiest case' is an unverified optimisation claim wearing the clothes of an explanation, and it is most dangerous when the conclusion is right almost everywhere, because then every plausibility check passes and only the one cell that matters is wrong. Prefer a measured artefact over a derived bound wherever one can be built: the 44-node skeleton settled in one gate run what the arithmetic had got wrong for a day. | applied | `tree_feasibility` corrected to the narrowest legal fan and re-run; `AL-442` and `UW-C272` carry the corrected count, the corrected widths, and the empirical counter-example |
| AL-452 | 2026-08-17 | Preparing the 8-11 story-first evaluation, checking whether the 5-8 re-cut remedy generalises | validator | **CG-3 measures only a stop that contains at least one no-decision node, so a fat standalone decision node escapes the words-per-stop ceiling entirely, and splitting prose to be finer-grained makes the finding WORSE rather than better.** Measured at 8-11: a single decision node carrying 200 words produces zero CG-3 findings, while the same material split into a 70-word no-decision node plus a 70-word decision, 140 words and 60 fewer, fires CG-3. The cause is that `_find_runs` yields runs of single-choice nodes, and a decision node with no run in front of it is never the head of any run, so it is never composed or measured. It is still a rendered stop: `compose_stop` starting on a decision returns that one node. The gap between the two ceilings is large, because PL-19's per-node max at 8-11 is 220 while the CG-3 stop ceiling is 135, so up to 220 words of stop are unmeasured. **This inverts `AL-447`'s remedy at the flowed bands.** Re-cutting a scene into beat-length nodes is what took the 5-8 story-first draft from failing to passing, because 3-5 and 5-8 render one node per page (ADR-026 decision 4) and only the per-node ceiling applies. At 8-11 and up the nodes re-compose into one stop, so the same re-cut converts an unmeasured node into a measured over-ceiling stop. At a flowed band the real constraint is not per node at all: it is that total prose BETWEEN two decisions must fit 135 words, which is a story property, not a bookkeeping one. | Measure every stop, not only the ones with a run in front of them: treat a decision or ending node with no preceding run as a stop of one and apply the same ceiling, which makes the rule say what its name says and removes the perverse incentive to merge nodes to escape it. Doing so will fire on existing content, so land it with the same grandfathering the rest of the CG family has rather than as a silent tightening. Second, correct the authoring guidance: 'decompose long scenes into beats' is right at 3-5 and 5-8 and wrong at 8-11 and up, where the fix is to decide more often. Generalised check: when a rule measures a composed unit, check what it does with the degenerate unit of size one; skipping it is the common bug and it usually inverts the rule's incentive, rewarding exactly the shape the rule exists to discourage. | open | |
| AL-453 | 2026-08-17 | Second story-first experiment, same method at 8-11, gated against the same bar and compared with the architecture-first skeleton in the same cell | product | **Two findings, and the first one retires a claim this log made yesterday.** (a) *Convergence is not the conflict.* `AL-447` concluded from one 5-8 story that a quality-first writer rejects reconvergence on craft grounds, which the strict bar makes compulsory above 5-8/short. The 8-11 story reconverges **five** times, unprompted, and the writer volunteered a per-decision ledger of where it merged paths and why, including admitting two merges were taken partly for economy rather than craft. So the earlier finding was an artefact of story LENGTH, not of craft: a 16-passage story can afford to keep every path separate and a 19-passage story spanning two acts cannot, and a good writer reconverges without being asked. The architecture's requirement matches what longer stories do anyway. (b) *The real conflict is decision density, and it is much larger than the granularity mismatch already logged.* At 8-11 every passage ends in a choice or an ending, so each passage IS one rendered stop. Measured: **0 of 19 stops fit the 135-word CG-3 ceiling**; the smallest is 1.9x it and the median, 439 words, is 3.3x. Against the architecture-first skeleton in the same cell, `the-half-hour-call`, whose 61 composed stops all fit at a median of 100 words, the story-first median stop is **4.4x larger**. Unlike the 5-8 case this cannot be fixed by re-cutting, because at a flowed band the pieces recompose into the same stop (`AL-452`); the only remedy is to decide more often, which is a change to the story. Put plainly, the framework wants a 9-year-old to meet a choice about every 135 words and a strong writer wanted one about every 440. | Withdraw the convergence half of `AL-447`'s framing and keep the units half; one story is not a sample, and the second one contradicted the first on exactly the axis the first was confident about. Then put the real number to the owner as a calibration question rather than an authoring one: **CORRECTED: this framing was wrong and the owner caught it.** The number is not an untested ceiling. ADR-011 section 10, adopted 2026-08-01 as decision D15 on owner sign-off from `design-review-kid-appeal-2026-08-01.md` section 8 with a cited research appendix (Fendt et al. 2012 ICIDS; Takacs, Swart and Bus 2015), gives an age-graded words-per-stop RANGE for every band: 10-40 at 3-5, 30-70 at 5-8, 60-135 at 8-11, 80-150 at 10-13, 100-200 at 13-16, 100-230 at 16+. The validator implements the top of each range exactly. So 135 is the top of a researched band, and the story-first draft's 439-word median is 3.3x that and roughly 2x the top of even the 16+ range, which means it is off-spec for every band rather than evidence against this one. The open question is therefore not whether the ceiling is calibrated but why a strong writer lands so far outside a researched range without being told it exists A 439-word stop is roughly three minutes between choices, a 135-word stop roughly one. Generalised check: when a generative process and a validator disagree by a factor, measure the factor before theorising about the cause, and re-run the experiment at a second point on the axis before generalising from the first; both of this log's confident story-first conclusions came from n=1 and one of them did not survive n=2. | open | |
| AL-454 | 2026-08-17 | Owner recalled that words-per-decision was researched into per-band ranges, which sent me to ADR-011 section 10 | validator | **ADR-011 section 10 sets a words-per-stop range for all six bands; CG-3 enforces it for four, and at the two it skips the substitute rule is about twice as permissive as the research.** At 3-5 and 5-8 a node IS a stop (ADR-026 decision 4), so section 10's range and `words_per_node_profile` govern the same quantity, and they disagree: section 10 says 10-40 words at 3-5 where the per-node advisory allows 28-55 and PL-19's hard max allows 90; section 10 says 30-70 at 5-8 where the advisory allows 50-95 and the max allows 155. `_WORDS_PER_STOP_CEILING` has no entry below 8-11, because the stop concept was implemented as a flowed-band idea, and section 10 is not: it rules on all six. Consequence measured immediately: the re-cut of the 5-8 story-first draft (`AL-447`) passes `--strict` with zero findings while sitting at a 89-word median with 37 of its 44 stops outside the researched 30-70 range. It is not a clean pass, it is an unmeasured one, and it was reported as clean. The four skeletons authored this run all sit inside their section 10 ranges, but by luck rather than by check: they were sized from the per-node advisory band, which happens to overlap the research at its top. | Give CG-3 entries for 3-5 and 5-8 from section 10's own numbers so the rule covers the bands the ADR covers, and reconcile `words_per_node_profile` with section 10 at the two discrete bands rather than leaving two tables ruling on one quantity with different answers, the looser of which is the enforced one. Then re-check the committed catalog against the corrected rule before it is treated as a bar. Generalised check: when an ADR rules on N cases and the validator implements M of them, the missing cases do not fail, they go unmeasured and then get reported as passing; and where a second table already constrains the same quantity, the gap is invisible because something plausible fires instead. Look for the substitute whenever coverage is partial. | open | |
| AL-455 | 2026-08-17 | Owner ruled that each band should have an allowed portion of no-decision nodes, and then that it must not starve paths of decisions | validator | **CG-1 was enforcing a local maximum where ADR-011 section 10 states an average, which forbade a legitimate long scene in a story whose overall cadence was well inside the research.** Section 10's cadence column reads 'choice every 2nd-4th page' at 3-5 and 'every 1st-2nd' at 5-8: an average over the story, not a per-run ceiling. The implemented rule was a run cap alone, so a single four-page scene blocked even when the story spent decisions freely elsewhere. Splitting the work fixes it: a per-band SHARE of non-ending nodes that may be single-choice carries the average, and the run cap stays as a local backstop against one endless corridor. The 5-8 run cap rises 2 to 3 in the same change, because a cap that binds before the share makes the share a dead letter. **The balance the owner then asked about is already held, and by a rule nobody had connected to this one.** The worry is that a permissive share lets a path run 400 words per decision. It cannot, at any band: PL-26 caps the fewest-decision shortest path at 6 nodes per decision everywhere, and above 5-8 CG-3 caps the composed stop at the band's words-per-stop range, 135 at 8-11 through 230 at 16+. So the two axes compose rather than fight, the share saying how many NODES may sit between decisions and CG-3 saying how many WORDS. 400 words per decision is unreachable at a flowed band without amending D15 itself, which is the lever this change deliberately does not touch. | Keep the three rules explicitly paired in the docs, because each is unreadable alone: the share is the average cadence, the run cap is the local backstop, and CG-3 plus PL-26 are the counterweight that stops the share from starving a path. Shares are derived rather than chosen: 0.75 at 3-5 and 0.50 at 5-8 come straight from section 10's pages-per-choice, and 0.50 at the flowed bands from the top of the words-per-stop range over the low end of the per-node band, which gives 1.8 to 2.1 nodes per stop. Generalised check: when a rule is stated as an average and implemented as a maximum, it will refuse legitimate work while still admitting the aggregate failure it was written to prevent; check which quantifier the source document used before implementing a bound. Second: a story with no decision at all has no cadence, so the share must skip it rather than report every such graph as infinitely over, which is PL-17's job instead. | applied | `_CHOICELESS_SHARE` added to `validator/choice_grammar.py` with per-band derivations recorded on the constant, `_DISCRETE_RUN_CAP['5-8']` raised 2 to 3, share finding and zero-decision guard added to `check_choiceless_run_cap`; `TestChoicelessShareAllowance` added and the 5-8 boundary tests updated; all six skeletons authored this run re-verified passing `--strict` |
| AL-456 | 2026-08-17 | Owner asked for a relaxed narrative rule at the first decision point, on the argument that ground has to be covered before a choice can mean anything | validator | **PL-25's anchor counts pages and PL-25 counts nodes, and the unit mismatch inverted BOTH of its bounds.** JHM 2019 Table 4 measures pages to the first decision (median 4, range 2 to 8.25); a page is a quantity of prose, while a node here is an authoring unit holding anywhere from a fifth of a page to two pages. So the floor failed a single 530-word opening scene while passing two 40-word stubs, and the ceiling failed an opening told in eight 40-word beats (320 words) while passing five 200-word pages (1,000 words). In each pair the rule blocked the version that serves its own stated purpose. This is the third instance of one defect class in two days, after `AL-452` (CG-3 measuring only multi-node stops) and `AL-454` (ADR-011 section 10's stop ranges implemented against a different table), and all three are the same mistake: a research quantity adopted as a threshold without checking that the implementation counts the thing the research counted. Second finding, from implementing it: the relaxation makes PL-25 read a PER-NODE property of the opening walk, and the breadth-first helper it used broke ties lexically, which is exactly the trap documented on that helper and the one PL-26 had already been bitten by. Equally short openings can carry very different word counts, so a rename could have flipped the verdict. Third: measured honestly, this relaxation unblocks nothing currently observed. All three story-first drafts fail PL-19's per-node max first, and a PL-19-legal re-cut of each opening lands inside the PL-25 node window anyway (5 nodes at 5-8, 4 at 8-11 and 10-13), so what actually binds a story-first draft is PL-19 forcing the split and CG-1 penalising the corridor it creates, not PL-25. A correct rule change and an unblocking rule change are not the same thing and should not be reported as one. | Read each bound in nodes OR in the words those nodes stand for, one-way, so no passing story can start failing; bracket equally short openings with a min and a max instead of choosing one path, so the verdict cannot depend on node ids. Then audit the remaining research-anchored thresholds (PL-20, PL-26, `_MIN_COMPLETE`) for the same pages-versus-nodes unit error before it is found a fourth time by accident. | applied | `_opening_extent`, `_OpeningExtent` and `_opening_in_word_window` added to `validator/policy.py` and wired into both PL-25 branches; the orphaned `_shortest_path_nodes` and `_shortest_path_to` helpers removed with their references retargeted; unit note added to `band_profile._FIRST_DECISION_DEPTH`; six PL-25 tests added to `tests/unit/test_policy.py` including a rename-invariance case; differential sweep over all 68 committed skeletons shows zero verdict changes |
| AL-457 | 2026-08-17 | Fourth story-first experiment, same method at 13-16, gated against the same bar and compared with the three earlier bands | product | **The falling gap between story-first drafts and the ADR stop range is an artefact of the denominator, not the generator adapting to age.** Median scene length across the four drafts is 246 (5-8), 439 (8-11), 400 (10-13), 279 (13-16): not monotone, no trend. The ratio against ADR-011 section 10's words-per-stop top falls cleanly (3.5x, 3.3x, 2.7x, 1.4x) only because that top rises with band while the writer's scene length does not move. So a quality-first model given the age rules writes at roughly adult scene granularity in every band, and the per-band stop range is carried entirely by the validator. Any authoring brief that does not state the band's stop range explicitly will get a draft cut at 250 to 440 words per scene whatever band it names. Second finding, and the sharper one: **at 13-16 the cell and the story nearly fit, and what remains is a tension between PL-17 and craft rather than a units mismatch.** Re-cut at 120 words per node the 14,002-word draft is 118 nodes, inside 13-16/medium's 115-170 budget, at 119 words per node inside the 100-185 advisory; blocking errors fall from 17 to 2. Both survivors say the graph is too shallow for its word count: PL-17 wants 18 endings against the story's 14, and PL-20 wants a 15-node satisfying arc against 12. But the writer's own unprompted report is that 14 endings at 200 to 350 words each already consumed about a third of the budget and compressed mid-branch scenes toward summary, and that ending count rather than branch depth was the binding constraint. PL-17's breadth-scaled floor therefore pushes for four MORE endings in a story whose author says endings were already crowding out the scenes that earn them. Third, confirming rather than new: the 13-16 draft reconverges six times unprompted, with the writer describing the cost in craft terms (a merged passage cannot name who is present, so the drowning ending loses its best image), which is the same conclusion `AL-453` reached at 8-11 and further evidence that `UW-C275`'s convergence half should not drive work. Fourth, a scope correction to `AL-456`: PL-25's relaxation DOES fire here, on the un-recut form (first decision at node 1 with 375 words against a floor of 2 nodes, or 280 words), which would have blocked before today and is clean now. The realistic re-cut form is 3 nodes deep and passes either way, so `AL-456`'s scope note stands: the relaxation is still not what unblocks a story-first draft. | State the band's words-per-stop range from ADR-011 section 10 directly in the authoring brief and the skeleton brief, since the generator demonstrably does not infer it from the age band. Separately, put the PL-17 versus craft-budget tension to the owner: a breadth-scaled endings floor that rises with node count assumes endings are cheap, and the measured cost here is 200 to 350 words each. | open | `UW-C278` |
| AL-458 | 2026-08-17 | Owner asked for a series of subagents to validate the implementation against the research and ADRs, after three unit-error defects in two days | validator | **The unit errors we went hunting were real but were the smaller half of what a systematic doc-versus-code audit found.** Seven read-only lanes, each pinned to named source documents and named implementation files, each required to DEMONSTRATE a divergence with a constructed case rather than argue it, and each required to report what it checked and found consistent so a clean lane could be told from a lane that stopped early. The unit errors confirmed: PL-20 counts nodes where its budget is words (a 180-word fastest finish passes, less prose than the two-node hollow win the ADR says the floor forbids), and PL-26's ceiling is nodes while its anchor is pages, one flat number spanning 4.4x in the measured quantity. But the larger findings were a different shape: **three rules that cannot fire at all** (`UW-C280`), **a gate that checks something other than what it advertises** (`UW-C281`), **a prompt that demands what the gate rejects in 17 of 18 cells** (`UW-C279`), and **two safety guards whose own `#CRITICAL` comments state a property they do not have** (`UW-C285`). Four method notes worth keeping. First, requiring a demonstration is what made the output usable: every finding above arrived with the input and the numbers, and I re-verified the top claim of all seven lanes myself before relaying any of them. Second, one lead I handed a lane was WRONG (`_MIN_COMPLETE` is not page counts; the values reproduce the ADR's own fastest-finish minutes column across all 18 cells) and the lane refuted it rather than confirming my prior, which is the signal that the demonstration requirement was doing real work. Third, two lanes independently reached the PL-17 versus ADR section 5 contradiction from different directions, which is worth more than either report alone and is why it is filed separately. Fourth, telling each lane which findings were already known BASELINE rather than target bought new ground instead of re-derivations. The recurring root cause across all seven lanes is that nothing in the review path asks whether a rule is capable of firing, or whether a threshold's unit matches its source. | Add a test class asserting each gated rule CAN fire, since three could not and no suite noticed. Record the unit each threshold is stated in, per `UW-C277`. Then work `UW-C279` through `UW-C285` in severity order, starting with the two graded 1. | open | `UW-C279`, `UW-C280`, `UW-C281`, `UW-C282`, `UW-C283`, `UW-C284`, `UW-C285` |
